## Document 039: 2026年8月经济数据点评：生产回稳、内需偏弱，政策协同待发力

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年8月经济数据点评：生产回稳、内需偏弱，政策协同待发力.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

www.lhratings.com  研究报告 1

生产回稳、内需偏弱，政策协同待发
力—2026 年 8 月经济数据点评 
联合资信 主权部  
8 月宏观经济呈现“生产端回暖、 需求端偏弱”的结构性特征。 工业增加值同比增长5.2%，
较 7 月加快 0.7 个百分点， 制造业PMI 回升至 49.8%， 产需两端同步改善； 出口同比增长25.0%，
AI 产业链支撑突出。内需方面，社零同比增长 0.4%，较 7 月进一步放缓；1—8 月固定资产投
资下降 7.2%，降幅扩大，房地产开发投资下降 19.9%，拖累仍在加深。CPI 同比上涨 0.8%，
PPI 同比上涨 3.8%， 剪刀差收窄但传导仍不畅。 金融数据呈现“社融增速回落、 信贷超预期收
缩”，实体融资需求偏弱。整体看，供给侧韧性犹存，需求修复远未到位， 后续需重点关注信
贷边际改善、专项债实物工作量转化及房地产销售企稳信号。

### Page 2

www.lhratings.com  研究报告 2 
8 月，宏观经济在政策发力与外部韧性共同作用下呈现“生产回稳、外需偏强、
内需分化”的格局。 
一、出口：高景气延续，AI 产业链贡献突出 
8 月， 按美元计价， 中国出口总值4014.4 亿美元， 同比增长25.0%， 前值为23.9%；
进口总值 2823.6 亿美元，同比增长 28.2%；贸易顺差 1190.85 亿美元，较去年同期增
加 180.79 亿美元。单月出口规模再度突破 4000 亿美元，环比 0.9%略强于季节性。 
出口的强劲表现高度集中于 AI 相关产业链。 8 月集成电路出口金额同比增长
129.8%， 自动数据处理设备及其零部件同比增长76.5%， 两者对出口增速的贡献度合
计超过四成。 高新技术产品出口额达1244.67 亿美元， 同比增长56.88%， 增速较7 月
进一步抬升。 同期韩国半导体出口增速高达209%， 印证了全球AI 投资周期对东亚电
子产业链的同步拉动。 
分区域看， 对美出口在低基数下大幅回升17.4 个百分点至 34.4%； 对东盟出口增
速仍处 30.2%的偏高水平； 对欧盟出口增速回落至6.6%， 需关注贸易限制措施是否升
级。

数据来源：海关总署、Wind，联合资信整理 
图 1  进出口情况（%，亿美元） 
二、内需：消费增速进一步放缓，投资降幅扩大，房地产拖累加深 
8 月社会消费品零售总额 39824 亿元，同比增长 0.4%，较 7 月的 0.6%进一步回
落；1—8 月累计同比增长 1.1%。 除汽车以外的消费品零售额8 月同比增长 2.5%，汽
车类消费的拖累仍然显著。服务消费保持相对韧性，1—8 月服务零售额增长 4.9%，
显著高于商品零售额的 1.0%。但服务消费在整体消费中的占比有限，尚不足以对冲
商品消费的持续疲弱。 
固定资产投资方面，1—8 月全国固定资产投资（不含农户）293092 亿元，同比

### Page 3

www.lhratings.com  研究报告 3 
下降 7.2%， 降幅较1—7 月的-6.7%扩大 0.5 个百分点。 扣除房地产开发投资后， 固定
资产投资下降 4.2%。分产业看，第三产业投资下降 9.9%，是主要拖累；第二产业投
资下降 2.9%。从资金端看，8 月新增专项债发行 5188 亿元，规模年内仅次于 6 月，
截至 8 月末累计发行 2.93 万亿元， 完成全年额度的66.5%。 专项债发行有所提速， 但
从投资数据尚未看到明显的实物工作量转化。 
房地产市场方面，1—8 月全国房地产开发投资 47979 亿元， 同比下降19.9%，降
幅较 1—7 月的-19.2%进一步扩大 0.7 个百分点；其中住宅投资 37017 亿元，下降
19.7%。 新建商品房销售面积同比下降 12.1%， 销售额下降13.0%。 投资端与销售端同
步走弱，房地产市场的筑底过程仍在延续。

数据来源：国家统计局、Wind，联合资信整理 
图 2  投资累计同比增速 
三、工业：生产回稳，新动能韧性凸显，但中小企业景气仍然偏弱 
8 月规模以上工业增加值同比增长 5.2%，较 7 月的 4.5%加快 0.7 个百分点，环
比增长 0.54%。1—8 月累计同比增长 5.3%。制造业 PMI 方面，8 月制造业采购经理
指数为 49.8%，较上月上升 0.6 个百分点，景气水平有所回升，但仍连续处于收缩区
间。生产指数和新订单指数分别为 50.4%和 50.6%，均回升至扩张区间，产需两端同
步改善。在调查的 21 个行业中，有 16 个行业 PMI 较上月上升，行业景气度改善的
覆盖面较广。 
结构分化特征依然突出。 装备制造业和高技术制造业PMI 分别为 51.4%和 52.9%，
持续位于扩张区间； 消费品行业和高耗能行业PMI 分别为 49.0%和 47.9%， 虽有所回
升但仍处于收缩区间。 分企业规模看， 大型企业PMI 为 50.6%， 升至临界点以上； 中
型企业 PMI 为 49.4%，较上月下降 0.3 个百分点；小型企业 PMI 为 47.9%，虽回升
0.5 个百分点但仍显著低于临界点。中小企业的景气修复明显滞后于大型企业，反映
出当前经济回暖的传导尚不均衡。

四、价格：CPI 温和回升，PPI 同比涨幅扩大，但价格信号的传导仍不均
衡 
8 月 CPI 同比上涨 0.8%，涨幅较 7 月的 0.5%扩大 0.3 个百分点；环比由上月下
降 0.1%转为上涨 0.4%。扣除食品和能源价格的核心 CPI 同比上涨 1.0%，较前值回
升。 
CPI 的回升主要受能源价格拉动。 受国际油价再度上涨影响， 国内汽油价格环比
由上月下降 10.7%转为上涨 7.2%， 能源价格同比涨幅由上月的0.6%扩大至 4.1%，影
响 CPI 同比上涨约 0.28 个百分点。 食品价格环比上涨0.4%， 鲜菜价格环比上涨5.5%，
猪肉价格环比上涨 1.3%，季节性因素贡献了部分涨幅。值得注意的是，受AI 算力需
求快速增长影响，移动电话机、平板电脑、数据存储设备价格环比分别上涨 2.3%、
2.1%和 2.1%，高技术产品价格的上行开始对 CPI 形成正向贡献。 
PPI 方面，8 月全国工业生产者出厂价格同比上涨 3.8%，涨幅较 7 月的 3.5%进
一步扩大；环比由上月下降 0.7%转为上涨 0.4%。PPI 同比涨幅扩大的驱动力来自两
方面： 一是输入性因素， 国际原油及有色金属价格上行带动石油开采、 精炼石油产品
制造价格环比分别上涨 10.4%和 4.1%； 二是产业转型升级带来的需求增加， 电子电路
制造价格环比上涨 3.5%，虚拟现实设备制造价格上涨 1.9%。 
PPI 与 CPI 的剪刀差虽有所收窄，但价格信号的传导仍然不畅。上游涨价集中于
能源和部分高技术相关领域，中下游传统制造业和消费品行业的价格传导依然有限。
价格改善的“点状”特征明显，尚未形成全面的通胀传导链条。

五、金融：社融增速回落，信贷超预期收缩，融资需求修复仍需时间 
8 月金融数据呈现“社融增速回落、信贷超预期收缩”的格局。8 月新增社会融
资规模 1.66 万亿元，同比少增 9083 亿元；社融存量同比增速为 7.2%，较 7 月末的
7.4%回落 0.2 个百分点。前八个月社融增量累计为 23.91 万亿元，比上年同期少 2.64
万亿元，其中对实体经济发放的人民币贷款同比少增 2.71 万亿元。 
信贷需求的疲弱尤为突出。8 月人民币贷款仅增加 600 亿元，同比少增 5300 亿
元， 规模显著低于季节性水平。从结构看：住户减少 2029 亿元， 同比多减2332 亿元，
其中短期贷款减少 1219 亿元、中长期贷款减少 822 亿元，居民消费与购房加杠杆意
愿双双走弱；企业增加 2600 亿元，同比少增 3300 亿元，其中短期贷款减少 1600 亿
元、中长期贷款增加 3200 亿元，企业资本开支意愿尚未修复。M2 同比增长 7.5%，
较 7 月的 7.7%回落 0.2 个百分点；M1 同比增长 4.1%， 较上月微升0.1 个百分点。M2
与 M1 增速的剪刀差虽略有收窄，但 M1 绝对增速仍处于低位，反映企业活期存款的
活化程度有限，实体经济的交易和投资意愿尚未实质性改善。 
政府债券仍是社融的主要支撑项。8 月末政府债券余额同比增长 13.5%，占社融
存量的比重升至 22.3%。 但信贷的偏弱表明， 私人部门的融资需求修复仍是当前金融
数据的核心短板。

政策层面， 供给侧的政策支持和产业升级动能正在发挥作用，但投资数据的滞后
改善需要时间，单靠利率下行难以快速扭转信贷需求不足。 后续需要重点观察三个变
量： 一是9 月信贷数据能否在季末效应和政策推动下出现边际改善； 二是专项债资金
能否在三季度末形成更明显的实物工作量， 拉动基建投资止跌； 三是房地产销售端能
否在“金九银十”传统旺季出现企稳信号。在内需自发修复动能仍然偏弱的背景下，
财政与货币政策的协同力度、 以及增量政策工具是否会在四季度前出台， 将是决定全
年经济走势的关键变量。

## Document 173: 8月经济数据跟踪：新经济拉动作用仍强，政策有待进一步发力

Source PDF: all report pdf/8月经济数据跟踪：新经济拉动作用仍强，政策有待进一步发力.pdf
Report source: 万联证券
Date: 2026-09-18
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

[Table_RightTitle] 
宏观研究|宏观跟踪报告

[Table_Title] 
新经济拉动作用仍强，政策有待进一步发力

[Table_ReportType] 
——8 月经济数据跟踪

[Table_ReportDate] 2026 年 09 月 18 日

[T able_Summary] ⚫ 事件：2026 年8 月，工业增加值当月同比增速上行0.7 个百分点
至 5.2%，环比上涨0.54%。固投累计同比由-6.7%降至-7.2%，制
造业、基建、房地产投资分别由-1.7%、-3.6%、-19.2%下降为-
2.3%、4%、19.9%，社零当月同比为0.4%，前值为0.6%。 
⚫ 工业生产回升，产销率同比上行，高技术产业生产保持高位。随
高温天气消退，台风对生产的影响逐步减小，生产快速修复。发
电量同比持续下滑，工业企业出口交货值回升，海外制造业景气
度较高，AI 产业链拉动，叠加“抢出口”效应带动出口上行。采
矿业、制造业增速上行，高技术产业保持领跑，对生产的拉动作
用仍强。消费品制造业增速整体上行，中游设备制造业生产韧性
仍强，上游原材料行业整体边际走弱，能化产业链为主要拉动，
下游高端制造生产高位放缓。服务业生产、预期指数持续回落。 
⚫ 投资下行斜率放缓，地产投资持续偏弱。固定资产投资分项均下
滑，建安工程跌幅继续加深，设备购置在政策扶持下持续上行。
地产投资持续下跌，新开工、竣工跌幅加深，施工持续小幅下
探。房地产销售“量跌价涨” ，二手房销售有所回暖，去库进程持
续。8 月地产政策陆续出台，来提振房地产市场。未来仍需关注
“金九银十”房地产市场销售情况。房企资金来源持续收紧，拿
地持续回落。自筹资金和国内贷款渠道持续承压，按揭贷款的边
际改善主要受二手房交易活跃及前期按揭滞后发放支撑，定金及
预收款回落主要受新房销售偏弱及新政对预售资金的调整影响。 
⚫ 基建增速放缓，随资金落地有望给四季度带来支撑。专项债发行
逐步提速，随着资金到位，下行速率明显放缓。随着政策效果逐
步显现， “六张网”建设提速，未来仍有充足的改善动能。 
⚫ 制造业投资回落，民间投资仍弱，高技术产业投资为主要拉动。
制造业投资部分受政策落地节奏滞后影响。海外需求保持韧性，
出口旺盛给制造业带来支撑。大规模设备更新政策推动下，设备
工器具购置同比增速由9%上行至9.3%，随着超长期特别国债资金
落地，财政支出投放加速，政策支撑效果将逐步显现。 
⚫ 消费增速继续放缓，服务、商品均有所回落。内需依然偏弱，农
村消费好于城镇。限额以上企业销售跌幅大于消费整体，大企业
经营相对承压，网上商品和服务零售占社零比重持续上行。必选
消费韧性较强，可选消费表现分化。金银珠宝类本月跌幅最大，
金价中枢抬升，美联储加息预期下，市场预期偏弱给相关消费带
来一定抑制，高基数也带来一定影响。化妆品类增速持续放缓，
文化办公用品类增速回落。通讯器材类本月增速上行幅度相对最
大。地产下游的家具、建材、家电增速低位反弹。 
⚫ 总结与展望：新经济支撑作用仍强，随政策逐步落地，四季度经
济有望边际改善。8 月经济整体持续回落，但下行速度明显放

事件 
2026年8月，工业增加值当月同比增速上行0.7个百分点至5.2%（市场预期4.7%），环
比上涨0.54%，前值为0.11%。1-8月，固定资产投资累计同比增速-7.2%（市场预期-
7.2%）， 前值-6.7%， 其中， 房地产开发投资累计同比增速回落0.7个百分点至-19.9%，
基建投资累计同比增速回落0.4个百分点至-4%，制造业投资增速累计同比回落0.6个
百分点至-2.3%。社会消费品零售总额同比增速由上月的0.6%回落为0.4%（市场预期
0.7%）。

1    工业生产回升，高技术产业生产增速保持高位 
工业生产增速超出市场预期， 产销率同比上行，工业企业出口回升， 发电量同比持续
下滑。8月， 工业增加值当月同比增速上行0.7个百分点至5.2%，高于市场预期的4.7%，
环比上涨0.54%， 前值为0.11%， 随高温天气消退， 台风对生产的影响逐步减小， 生产
快速修复。发电量同比由上月的-0.1%回落至-0.8%，为2022年6月以来新低。工业企
业出口交货值当月同比由上月的10.4%回升至11.1%，海外制造业景气度较高，AI产业
链拉动， 叠加 “抢出口” 效应带动出口上行。规模以上工业企业产销率当月同比由上
月的-0.6%上涨至-0.1%。 
采矿业、 制造业增速上行， 高技术产业保持领跑， 对生产的拉动作用仍强。分大类看，
8月， 采矿业同比上行2.8个百分点至-1.4%， 原煤产量同比由7月的-10.1%收敛至-7.7%，
天然气产量同比上行1.7个百分点至0.8%，石油和天然气开采业同比小幅上行，国际
油价再度攀升， 行业生产保持旺盛。 制造业同比由上月的5.5%上行至6.1%； 高技术产
业同比由上月的16.9%小幅回落至16.7%，装备制造业同比增速由 12.3%小幅回落至
12.1%， 保持领跑制造业整体表现。 电力、 燃气及水的生产和供应业同比回落0.1个百
分点至4.9%。

际走弱， 能化产业链为主要拉动， 下游高端制造生产高位放缓。分行业来看， 上游原
材料加工制造业表现分化， 黑色金属冶炼及压延工业增速反弹， 而非金属矿物制品业

个百分点至5.2%，上行幅度相对最大；食品制造业、农副食品加工业增速均上行。

2    投资：固投下行斜率放缓，设备购置继续上行 
固定资产投资增速进一步下探，但下行斜率放缓，地产、 基建、 制造业增速均下滑，
建安工程跌幅继续加深，设备购置在政策扶持下持续上行。2026年8月，固定资产投
资累计同比增速-7.2%，市场预期-7.2%，前值-6.7%， 固投季调环比增速为-0.5%，前
值为-1.37%。虽然固定资产投资持续下滑， 但较前期有所减速， 政策出台效果正逐步
显现。其中，房地产开发投资累计同比增速回落0.7个百分点至-19.9%；基建累计同
比增速回落0.4个百分点至-4%， 制造业累计同比增速回落0.6个百分点至-2.3%， 民间
固定资产投资回落0.7个百分点至-10.1%。第一产业固定投资累计同比下滑幅度最大，
第二、 三产业同比跌幅继续加深。设备工器具购置同比增速由9%上行至9.3%，大规模
设备购置政策效果持续显现；建筑安装工程同比由-9.2%回落至-9.8%， 投资项目不足，
新开工放缓的影响持续。

2.1  房地产投资持续下跌，地产销售“量跌价涨” 
地产投资持续偏弱， 新开工、 竣工跌幅加深， 施工持续小幅下探。房地产投资累计同
比增速回落0.7个百分点至-19.9%，今年以来持续下行。房屋新开工面积累计同比增
速回落0.8个百分点至-24.8%，房屋施工面积累计同比由-12.7%下降为-12.8%，竣工
增速由-23.2%回落至-23.7%。 
房地产销售“量跌价涨” ，二手房销售有所回暖，去库进程持续。新建商品房销售面
积累计同比由-11.8%继续下探至-12.1%， 商品房销售额由-13.1%上行为-13%，房地产
销售量、 价走势分化， 一、 二线城市为主要拉动给量价分化带来一定影响。从70城房
价数据来看， 部分三四线城市新建住宅价格环比小幅上行， 但北京本月环比回落， 同
比整体依然偏弱； 二手房价格环比上行幅度放缓， 同比均持续下跌， 当前房价尚未企
稳。本月国家统计局新公布了二手房交易网签面积数据，同比增速达到10.6%，当前

[Table_Pagehead] 
证券研究报告|宏观报告

万联证券研究所 www.wlzq.cn 第 7 页 共 12 页  
新房销售相对偏弱， 而二手房销售数据显示有所回暖。住宅待售面积累计同比由-0.8%
下跌至-1.1%，其中住宅累计同比由0%下行至-0.5%，房地产去库进程持续推进。8月
为地产销售淡季， 地产政策陆续出台， 公积金提取扩容覆盖面扩大； 现房销售制度改
善，直至竣工验收具备交付条件后方可收取购房资金和按揭贷款， 优化了居民购房环
境。 北京、 上海核心城市购房条件进一步放松， 来提振房地产市场。 未来仍需关注 “金
九银十”房地产市场销售兑现情况，以及二手房成交价格能否企稳。

资料来源：Wind，万联证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，万联证券研究所

房企资金来源持续收紧，自筹资金和国内贷款渠道持续承压， 房企拿地持续回落。房
地产开发资金来源增速由-20.3%继续下探至-21%。其中， 自筹资金和国内贷款跌幅相
对较大， 房企资质偏弱影响下， 相关资金来源受阻。 定金和预收款同比由-14.4%回落
为-14.8%，而个人按揭贷款跌幅持续收窄，按揭贷款的边际改善主要受二手房交易活
跃及前期按揭滞后发放支撑 ，定金及预收款回落主要受新房销售偏弱及新政对预售
资金的调整影响。100大中城市土地成交来看，8月房企拿地跌幅进一步加深， 房地产
行业去库存进程下，叠加预售资金制度改革影响，房企资金周转长期来看将受到约束，
预期房企拿地进程持续偏缓。

2.2  基建增速放缓，后续项目资金到位有望边际改善 
基建增速持续放缓，随资金落地有望给四季度带来支撑。基建累计同比增速回落0.4
个百分点至-4.0%，专项债发行逐步提速，随着资金到位，下行速率明显放缓。随着
政策效果逐步显现， “六张网”建设提速，未来仍有充足的改善动能。 
基建各分项增速集体回落，交运、 仓储行业保持韧性。从细分项来看， 电力、 热力、
燃气及水的生产和供应业均进一步下行， 随着高基数效应减弱，和财政支出落地，四
季度有望边际抬升。交通运输、仓储、邮政业回落幅度最小，依然保持正增；水利、
环境、公共设施管理业增速下行1.9个百分点至-11%，跌幅相对最大。

图表12：基建投资回落，水泥产量同比下滑（%） 图表13：水利、环境和公共设施管理业跌幅较大 (%)

资料来源：Wind，万联证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，万联证券研究所

2.3  制造业增速回落，高技术产业投资为主要支撑 
制造业投资放缓，民间投资仍弱，高技术产业投资为主要拉动。制造业投资累计同比
增速回落0.5个百分点至-2.3%，部分受政策落地节奏滞后影响。民间固定资产投资回
落0.7个百分点至-10.1%，高技术产业投资累计同比由5%上行至5.2%，为制造业投资
主要拉动项。 海外需求保持韧性， 出口旺盛给制造业带来支撑。大规模设备更新政策
推动下，设备工器具购置同比增速由9%上行至9.3%，随着超长期特别国债资金落地，
财政支出投放加速，政策支撑效果将逐步显现。 
图表14：出口韧性给制造业带来支撑（%） 图表15：企业融资环境指数持续回落（%）

资料来源：Wind，万联证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，万联证券研究所

3    消费增速继续放缓，服务、商品消费均有所回落 
消费增速继续下行，内需依然偏弱，餐饮、商品零售均放缓，农村消费好于城镇。8
月，社会消费品零售总额同比增速由上月的0.6%回落为0.4%，市场预期0.7%；其中，
除汽车以外的消费品零售增速持平于上月的2.5%；限额以上企业零售同比由上月的-
3.4%下跌至-3.7%，大企业经营依然相对承压。餐饮同比由上月的1.4%下跌至1.1%；
商品零售由0.5%降至0.3%。 城镇消费同比增速由0.5%降至0.2%， 乡村消费同比持平于
1.6%。 商品网上零售累计同比增速由4.6%降至4.3%， 网上商品和服务零售占社零比重
持续上行。 从更新到7月的消费者信心数据来看，消费者信心、收入指数继续回落，
消费意愿指数与上月持平。

图表16：消费增速继续下滑（%） 图表17：网上零售和服务占社零比重稳步上行（%）

资料来源：Wind，万联证券研究所 资料来源：Wind，万联证券研究所

暑期给线下消费带来支撑，可选消费表现分化， 必选消费韧性较强。可选消费来看，
金银珠宝类本月跌幅最大， 金价中枢抬升，美联储加息预期下，市场预期偏弱给相关
消费带来一定抑制， 高基数也带来一定影响。 化妆品类增速持续放缓，文化办公用品
类增速回落。通讯器材类本月增速上行幅度相对最大。地产下游的家具、 建材、 家电
增速低位反弹。 汽车消费同比跌幅继续下探， 消费补贴和税费优惠政策退坡带来一定
影响。 原油价格持续走高， 石油及制品类零售同比受到抑制， 但假期出行的提振作用
下， 跌幅边际收窄。 必选消费品中， 烟酒类涨幅最大， 基数走低带来一定影响， 粮油、
食品类、中西药品类增速回落，饮料类、服饰类、日用品增速均上行。

4    失业率上行，毕业季带来一定干扰 
失业率上行，大学生毕业季带来季节性扰动。城镇失业率较7月上行0.1个百分点至
5.3%，31个大城市城镇失业率由7月的5.2%上行至5.3%。外来农业户籍人口失业率从
4.9%小幅上行至5.0%， 就业人员平均工作时间持平于48.2小时/周。 从BCI企业招工前
瞻指数来看，由51.51%回落为50.98%，企业对于招工预期持续回落。7月为大学生集
中毕业时点，失业率季节性上行，8月就业的滞后影响仍存，随着就业逐步被吸纳，
四季度有望边际好转。

图表19：失业率小幅抬升（%） 图表20：7月年轻群体失业率显著抬升(%)

5    总结与展望 
新经济支撑作用仍强，随政策逐步落地，四季度经济有望边际改善。8月经济整体持
续回落，但下行速度明显放缓， 政策落地效果正逐步显现。结构分化持续， 新经济拉
动明显。随着财政资金投放陆续到位，新型政策性金融工具9月开启发行，仍将给经
济带来支撑，“六张网”将成为四季度稳投资的重要载体，基建仍将发挥重要的压舱
石的作用。

6    风险提示 
地缘政治风险发展超预期，居民购房、消费持续偏弱，政策推进和落地不及预期。

## Document 190: ACCI-Westpac Business Survey Q3 2026 (2026-09-18)

Source PDF: all report pdf/ACCI-Westpac Business Survey Q3 2026 (2026-09-18).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-09-18
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

ACCI–Westpac
Survey of Industrial Trends
Australian Chamber of Commerce and Industry & Westpac Banking Corporation
260th report September 2026 (survey conducted from 20 July to 3 September 2026)
 Momentum in Australia's manufacturing sector remained sluggish through Q3, with the Westpac-ACCI
Actual Composite edging slightly lower from 50.5 in Q2 to 49.1 in Q3, consistent with a neutral-to-slightly-
contractionary reading.
 Manufacturers are contending with numerous headwinds at present. As the Middle East conflict has continued

to unfold, the combination of elevated inflation, higher interest rates and lingering uncertainty has made for a
more challenging environment for consumer demand and some parts of the construction sector.
 Growth in new orders remained absent in Q3, with a net 2% of respondents reporting a decrease after only a
net 1% reported an increase in Q2. Sluggish demand saw a similar net percentage of manufacturers wind back
production, reduce headcount and decrease overtime. Manufacturers instead chipped away at existing order

backlogs and built up some inventory in anticipation of future increases.
 Spillovers from the Middle East conflict continue to play a significant role when it comes to cost pressures.
A net 48% of respondents reported an increase in average unit costs during Q3, barely changed from the net
51% reporting an increase in Q2. Pressures remain particularly acute in sub-sectors with more direct exposure
to fuel, fertiliser and other supply chain stresses, including machinery and equipment, fabricated and primary

metals, chemicals, wood and furniture.
 Despite persistent cost pressures, the proportion of firms reporting an increase in selling prices fell to a
net 24% in Q3. This remained well below the proportion reporting higher average unit costs, suggesting
manufacturers continue to absorb part of the increase in costs rather than fully passing them on to customers,
placing pressure on margins.
 The Westpac-ACCI Expected Composite rose from 52.7 in Q2 to 57.9 in Q3, indicating improved confidence

in future activity. Stronger expectations for new orders and output suggest manufacturers expect the current
period of soft demand to be relatively short lived.
 However, while manufacturers may see some light at the end of the tunnel for demand and production, they
do not see a significant let-up in cost pressures anytime soon, with a net 43% anticipating a further increase
in average unit costs over the next three months. Ongoing margin squeeze is the baseline view among

manufacturers, contributing to a weaker outlook for profit growth.
 Manufacturers remain somewhat cautious about their investment plans for the year ahead. On balance, a net
14% of firms intend to increase plant and equipment spending over the next twelve months, while a net 4%
intend to increase building investment. While expectations have turned more bullish in some areas, until such
outcomes materialise, some level of hesitancy may persist.

The Survey of Industrial Trends produced by the Australian
Chamber of Commerce and Industry & Westpac Banking
Corporation is a quarterly publication.
It is Australia's longest running business survey dating
from 1966, providing a timely update on manufacturing and

insights into economy-wide trends.

Email: economics@westpac.com.au

Ryan Wells, Economist
Lonergan Research
Net response or "balance" is calculated by the proportion of
"ups" less "downs" on individual questions, thereby yielding
the net balance. A positive balance indicates a net upward
or improving trend and a minus balance a net downward or
deteriorating trend.

The 260th consecutive survey was closed on Monday, x
September 2026 A total of 413 responses were received,
and provided a reasonable cross–section of Australian
manufacturing in respect of industry groups and size of
operation.

3
Key Survey Results
September 2026
• Manufacturing sentiment about the general business
outlook deteriorated sharply last quarter, as manufacturers
were confronted with acute cost pressures and a stalling in
new orders growth.
• Sentiment remains pessimistic but conditions improved

in Q3, with a net 12% of respondents expect the general
business situation to deteriorate over the next six months,
compared to a net 21% anticipating a deterioration in Q2.
• While manufacturers are expecting an improvement in
demand and production near-term, the backdrop of rising
costs, higher interest rates and difficulties in sourcing
labour will continue to present challenges for businesses.
• Momentum in Australia's manufacturing sector remained

sluggish in Q3, with the Westpac-ACCI Actual Composite
edging slightly lower from 50.5 to 49.1, consistent with a
neutral-to-slightly-contractionary reading.
• Underpinning the result were small declines across all of
the constituent components: output, new orders, overtime,
numbers employed and order backlogs.
• In contrast, the Westpac-ACCI Expected Composite
rose from 52.7 in Q2 to 57.9 in Q3, indicating improved
confidence in future activity. However, recent history has

shown that expectations can run ahead of actual future
outcomes for a period of time, and that could remain the
case should current headwinds persist.
• The Labour Market Composite held steady moving into the
second half of the year, nudging only slightly lower from
44.7 in Q2 to 44.5 in Q3.
• Results in the Labour Market Composite have foreshadowed
the trends in official labour market data for the market
sector, where jobs growth started to recover last year and

kick into a higher gear earlier this year.
• More recently, the Labour Market Composite has pointed to
a renewed slowing in jobs growth momentum. This would
be consistent with a moderation in labour demand over the
coming quarters as the effects of slower economic growth
from high inflation and interest rates continue to weigh on
the activity.

### Page 4

4
Manufacturing & the Business Cycle
• The Westpac-ACCI Actual Composite has a solid track
record as a timely indicator of near-term domestic economic
conditions and turning points in the cycle.
• The Q2 National Accounts showed that growth is slowing
but not as rapidly as initially feared. Underneath that is a

complex mix of headwinds and tailwinds. There are clear
drags coming from higher interest rates, elevated inflation,
slowing public investment and the housing market (albeit
so far confined to the direct effects from lower housing
turnover). However, these are being offset by tailwinds
related to firmer business investment, particularly in data
centres, energy and related infrastructure.
• This survey suggests softer demand and lingering cost
pressures remain a persistent themes that will continue to

shape momentum in local manufacturing and the domestic
economy over the coming months.
The Business Cycle & Economic Outlook
Manufacturing & Business Investment
• The ACCI-Westpac survey has historically provided a broad
gauge on equipment spending trends in the manufacturing
sector. However, actual spending has persistently under
performed the investment intentions in this survey,
suggesting that some plans are failing to materialise.
• Official data had been turning more constructive, but

momentum looks to have slipped once again. The National
Accounts showed that an investment pulse is underway in
Australia, but it is being chiefly driven by a few sectors –
particularly data centres and renewables. Outside of these
sectors, growth in equipment spending is more subdued.
• Ongoing uncertainty surrounding the Middle East conflict,
together with elevated cost pressures, higher interest
rates and a subdued demand outlook are likely to remain a

headwind for investment.
Australian & World Manufacturing Surveys
• Global and Australian manufacturing cycles tend to be
synchronised, especially at major turning points. The
Westpac-ACCI Actual Composite typically moves in line
with global manufacturing conditions, albeit with some
level of variation within any given cycle.
• However, more recently, trends have started to diverge
across the major manufacturing hubs. The US continues to
weather the headwinds associated with tariff uncertainty

and the US-Iran war, with the ISM PMI at 54.6 in August,
firmly in expansionary territory. It is a similar story in
Europe, where the S&P Global PMI has risen to 52.7 in
August, its highest in many years. Meanwhile, China's
manufacturing remains relatively lacklustre, similar to
Australia, with the NBS PMI around breakeven territory at
49.8 as of August.

5
Activity & Orders
• Growth in new orders remained sluggish in Q3, with a net
2% of respondents reporting a decrease, easing from a net
1% reporting an increase in Q2.
• As the Middle East conflict continues, the combination
of elevated inflation, higher interest rates and lingering

uncertainty has made for a more challenging environment
for consumer demand and some parts of the construction
sector.
• Manufacturers expect growth in new orders to improve
over the next three months, with a net 20% anticipating an
increase. Whether these expectations will be met given the
challenging backdrop is a key question.

Exports
Q2 2026 Q3 2026
Actual – net balance 9 8

• Growth in manufacturing exports were broadly stable
moving into the second half of the year, with a net 8% of
firms reporting an increase in Q3, following Q2's net 9%.
• Demand from our Asian trading partners have continued to
support processed food and agriculture exports. However,
a firmer AUD and uncertainty from the Middle East conflict

would have dampened growth in the quarter. US tariff risks
meanwhile remain a wildcard, although fortunately, it has
had little overall impact to date.
• Expectations have cooled compared to earlier in the year
but they remain positive, with a net 12% expecting an
increase in exports over the next three months.
• After slowing materially last quarter, output took another
step lower in Q3. A net 2% of firms reported a decrease in
output, the first decline in a year.

• Demand remained sluggish during the quarter, leading
manufacturers to chip away at existing order backlogs and
build up some inventory in anticipation of future increases.
• Looking ahead, a net 26% of firms expect an increase in
output over the next three months, suggesting the current
soft patch is expected to only be temporary. However, it is
important to note that expectations have been more bullish
than actual future outcomes over the past two years.

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
6
• Manufacturers turned more cautious about their investment
plans for the year ahead, following the onset of the Middle
East conflict.
• That broadly remained the case in Q3, though investment
intentions were still positive. On balance, a net 14% of
firms intend to increase plant and equipment spending over

the next twelve months, while a net 4% intend to increase
building investment.
• There remains a significant degree of uncertainty around
the economic outlook. Expectations for production and
orders have turned more bullish, but until such outcomes
materialise, some level of hesitancy might persist.
Investment & Profitability
• Profit expectations took a substantial hit last quarter,
reflecting a combination of surging cost pressures, higher
interest rates and the onset of the Middle East conflict.

This saw a sharp increase in cost pressures and substantial
uncertainty around the outlook.
• With these headwinds persisting, the outlook for profits
was little changed. Only a net 10% of firms expect an
improvement in profits over the coming year, below the
historic average of a net 19%.
• While manufacturers are anticipating an improvement in
new orders over the coming months, cost pressures are
also expected to remain acute, compressing margins.

• Capacity utilisation has quickly swung from a period of
overutilisation earlier this year, prior to the onset of the
Middle East conflict, back toward underutilisation.
• In the latest survey, fewer firms reported operating at
above average capacity (23% to 18%) with more firms
reporting that they were operating at below average
capacity (25% to 28%). This saw an increase in the net
balance of firms reporting capacity underutilisation, up from
2% in Q2 to 10% in Q3.

• This is still a fairly robust result. Over the full history
of the survey, manufacturers have tended to report a
larger degree of underutilisation, especially during cycles
characterised by slower growth and/or higher interest
rates.

7
• Employment dynamics in the manufacturing sector remain
sluggish. A net 2% of firms reported a decline in the size
of the workforce in Q3, following on from a net 4% also
reporting a pull-back in Q2.
• Manufacturers were confronted with a stagnation in new
orders growth and pull-back in production during the

quarter, providing little scope to increase headcount.
• Over the next three months however, a net 7% of
firms expect to increase headcount. While modest, the
expected lift in employment coincides with manufacturers'
expectations for a rebound in new orders and production
over the coming months.
The Labour Market
• Historically, firms' views on the difficulty of finding labour
tracks shifts in the Australian unemployment rate, albeit
with some variation between cycles depending on the

industry mix of employment growth.
• In Q3, a larger share of manufacturers reported difficulty
in sourcing labour, with a net 28.3% indicating labour was
"harder to find" compared to three months ago.
• Overall jobs growth has held up well so far this year, but
the spill overs from the Middle East conflict and recent
interest rate rises are expected to weigh on jobs growth
over the coming months.
• Manufacturers' use of overtime deteriorated further in Q3,

with a net 3% reporting a decline, in line with expectations
from the prior quarter.
• Hours worked usually adjusts more quickly to shifts in
demand conditions than employment. The fact that both
eased materially at the same time points to a more abrupt
cooling in labour demand within the sector.
• Manufacturers anticipate a modest improvement in
overtime usage over the coming three months, with a net
5% expecting an increase. If manufacturers' expectations

for demand crystallise, some increase in overtime would be
required to avoid building up order backlogs.

### Page 8

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
8
• Despite persistent unit cost pressures, the proportion of
firms reporting an increase in selling prices fell slightly
during Q3, to a net 24%.
• This was well below the net 48% reporting an increase in
average unit costs over the same period. It also fell well
short of manufacturers' own expectations from last quarter,

implying margins are being squeezed as firms only partially
pass through higher costs onto customers.
• Manufacturers expect only partial pass-through of higher
costs to customers will remain the case over the next
few months, with a net 27% expecting to increase selling
prices.
Prices & Inflation
• Results around wage expectations in the manufacturing
sector were broadly unchanged versus last quarter, with a
net 31% indicating they expect their next enterprise wage

agreement to delivery an outcome above the last.
• The survey suggests no real step-change in expectations
following the Fair Work Commission's 2026 Annual Wage
Review, which announced a larger-than-expected 4.75%
increase in award wages in Q2, to be implemented during
Q3. Expectations are still elevated, and this may compound
existing non-labour cost pressures for many manufacturers.
• Consistent with this survey, official ABS data points to a
turnaround in the wages growth trend, up to 3.3%yr in Q2,

slightly outstripping the national average of 3.2%yr.
• Manufacturers are grappling with acute cost pressures as
the supply shock stemming from the Middle East continues
to impact. In Q3, a net 48% of firms reported a rise in
average unit costs.
• Sub-sectors with more direct exposure to fuel, energy
intensive goods such as fertilisers, plastics and metals are
reporting more acute pressure, including machinery and
equipment, fabricated and primary metals, chemicals, wood

and furniture.
• Manufacturers do not see a significant let-up in cost
pressures near-term, with a net 43% anticipating a further
increase in average unit costs over the next three months.

Factors Limiting Production
Single Factor Most Limiting Production
Q1 2026 Q2 2026 Q3 2026
Orders (%) 29 35 36
Capacity (%) 11 10 8
Labour (%) 24 17 20
Finance (%)   12 10 12
Materials (%) 12 14 8
Other (%) 4 5 5
None (%) 8 9 11
• Orders remain an important constraint on production – a

lack of demand saw 36% of respondents cite this as the
single factor most limiting production. However, this is still
well below historical averages, showcasing the relative
importance of other supply-side limitations currently.
• Labour remains a clear example, with 20% citing it as the
primary constraint in Q3, above Q2's 17% and well above
the pre-pandemic average. Difficulties in sourcing labour
remains a persistent theme.
• Material shortages appear to have eased, with the

proportion of respondents citing it as the most limiting
factor down from 14% to 8%. While off its recent highs, it
is still well above the pre-pandemic average.
• Capacity dipped slightly to 8% in Q3, but the proportion
of respondents citing None has steadily risen over recent
quarters, up to 11% in Q3, suggesting that despite recent
disruptions and volatility, a decent portion of manufacturers'
production is returning back to some kind of 'norm'.

The Westpac–ACCI Composite Indices
The Westpac–ACCI Actual and Expected Composite indices are weighted averages of the activity measures in the survey. The 
weights are as follows: employment 20%; new orders 30%; output 25%; order backlog 15%; overtime 10% .
The Westpac–ACCI Labour Market Composite is a function of actual employment, with a weight of almost 50%, as well as: 
expected employment; expected overtime; new orders; order backlog; and expected order backlog.

2 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Moderate Growth, Broadening Opportunities
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
Although the Citi Economic Surprise Index has been easing over the past month as a
rising number of data releases have fallen short of elevated expectations (Exhibit 1), the
data remains broadly positive and consistent with sustained moderate growth. Indeed,
with alternating waves of headwinds and tailwinds, real consumer spending and gross

domestic product (GDP) continue to expand near their longer-run average rates even
with substantial wealth effects and strong growth in government transfer payments.
Employment and hours worked have barely increased over the past year, leaving
productivity to account for nearly all the economic growth. Combined with moderating
wage growth, this has contained unit labor costs—a positive for inflation dynamics.
Despite stubborn readings above the Federal Reserve's (Fed) 2% target inflation through

July, consumer price index data were relatively favorable, and although the latest personal
consumption expenditures (PCE) report was less reassuring, much of the upside pressure
remained concentrated in healthcare, tariff- and energy-sensitive categories, as well as
in volatile financial services that tend to follow equity-market gains. The latter inflation
category is slated to be revised lower when new index methodologies are announced
next month. Meanwhile, a broad range of other goods and services showed signs of

cooling inflation on a three-month annualized basis, as shown in Exhibit 2.

Basically, tariffs, elevated oil prices, wealth effects and AI-related electronics price
inflation continue to put upward pressure on inflation and interest rates. Although higher
gasoline prices have largely offset the One Big Beautiful Bill Act’s (OBBBA) direct benefit
to consumers, the shift from a negative fiscal impulse in 2025 to a positive impulse this
year has supported aggregate demand, contributing to the stalled progress on headline
inflation. Government transfers increased rapidly in July to a new high as a share of

personal income, as aging baby boomers retire.
Upside pressures on inflation have been counteracted in large part by slowing wage
growth, subdued housing activity, relatively tame credit growth, strong productivity, and
soft unit labor cost increases. In our view, if demand materially exceeded productive
capacity, inflation reacceleration would be much more pervasive.
Also important, the effective tariff rate is expected to average approximately 10%

this year, broadly matching its 2025 average absent another policy shock. The tariff
contribution to inflation is thus expected to continue to fade from its estimated
early-2026 peak of roughly 0.8 percentage point. Further oil-price moderation and
methodological changes affecting financial-service prices and other categories seen
as misrepresenting true inflation will also help improve the picture. Alongside anchored
longer-term inflation expectations, this suggests a gradual further moderation in

inflation, which would improve household purchasing power and support economic

### Page 3

3 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
growth. Real consumer spending appears on track for approximately 2.5% to 3% annualized
growth in Q3, following + 0.5% in Q1 and +3.5% in Q2.
Also positive, business investment in equipment and intellectual property accelerated strongly
in the first half. Business investment in manufactured goods is particularly positive for
broadening economic growth given manufacturing activity’s high multiplier effect on jobs and

aggregate demand. Helped by surging AI investment, OBBBA tax incentives, and rebounding
profitability, manufacturing activity is rapidly improving after several weak years, helping
sustain real GDP growth near 2.1% year-over-year, in line with its 25-year average. Although
July “core” capital-goods orders were disappointing, June was revised substantially higher,
raising estimates for Q3 business investment. Strong regional manufacturing surveys also

point to strong momentum, mostly the direct result of surging AI-related investment.
While there is no single accepted estimate for AI investment in the U.S., it is generally
projected at around $600 billion in 2026, $850 billion in 2027, $950 billion in 2028, and over
$1 trillion in 2029. While reaching new highs, its growth rate—and therefore its contribution
to GDP growth—is eventually projected to moderate from +50% to +60% in 2026 and 2027
to +10% by 2029. Also important, its domestic impact is smaller than the headline total

suggests. According to industry estimates, 70% of AI spending is directed toward equipment,
most of it imported. Thus, only around 50% of projected U.S. AI investment is poised to
directly help the domestic economy—though still enough to help broaden growth through
expanding domestic data-center construction, equipment production, power generation, and
supporting infrastructure.
The manufacturing sector’s broader spillovers to suppliers, construction, transportation, and

services make the sector’s recovery an important support for continued expansion. Consistent
with surging AI capital expenditures, rebounding industrial production, and very strong profit
estimates both here and abroad, BofA’s Global Wave 1 indicator has continued to increase—a
typically favorable indicator for cyclical assets’ performance relative to defensives.
With the growth and inflation outlook remaining positive, concerns have shifted to fiscal

strains and uncertainty, as the 2026 deficit appears likely to exceed the Congressional Budget
Office’s February projection by about $200 billion, and publicly-held debt is projected to
rise from approximately 101% of GDP in 2026 to 120% by 2036—a path that could test
investor confidence.
However, since there is no identifiable debt threshold that automatically triggers a crisis,
and comparisons with smaller or externally indebted economies are imperfect, the loss of

confidence in the sustainability of the debt has been short-lived. Indeed, U.S. debt is dollar-
denominated and approximately 70% is domestically held. Deep capital markets and the dollar’s
global reserve currency role sustain structural Treasury demand. Also important, healthy private
sector balance sheets and productivity growth leave scope for gradual fiscal consolidation in
a solidly growing economy without the dire negative effects on the economy observed in past

debt crises overseas. Thus, despite recent concerns, Treasury yields remain within their three-
year range and broadly consistent with moderate growth and anchored inflation expectations,
their recent variations largely tracking energy prices and geopolitical developments.
Also important, the term premium has returned to pre-global-financial-crisis levels following
exceptionally low levels in the post-2008-2009 decade. However, its increase appears to

be just a normalization in a delayed response to previous monetary tightening. Indeed, the
estimated 10-year term premium—or compensation required by investors to hold long-term
debt rather than roll over short-term debt—has historically followed the federal funds rate
with a substantial lag of almost 3 years.
While government debt supply, central-bank balance sheet policy, inflation, and interest-rate
volatility shape the term premium, the lagged fed funds–term premium correlation suggests

that the 2024 to 2025 policy easing is likely to act as a moderating force on the term premium
for the foreseeable future. In our view, the growth and interest rate environment is, therefore,
likely to remain constructive for continued expansion and cyclical assets’ performance,
including materials and infrastructure investments while fiscal uncertainty, soft labor demand,
and an eventually moderating AI investment impulse argue for portfolio diversification.

1 BofA Global Wave quantifies global economic trends, and it is used to forecast stock market performance.

### Page 4

4 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Taking Stock of the End of Capital Abundance
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
For much of the past quarter century, the global economy enjoyed a remarkable luxury:
Capital was abundant, cheap and seemingly limitless.
Savings flowed freely across borders. Global interest rates drifted steadily lower and,
for a time, actually turned negative across parts of Europe and Japan. Governments

borrowed with few apparent consequences. Corporations financed growth at historically
attractive rates. Households gained access to inexpensive credit. And investors prospered
as declining discount rates helped push the valuations of stocks, bonds, real estate and
other financial assets higher.
Few countries benefited more from this age of capital abundance than the U.S. As the
world's largest debtor nation and home to the world's deepest capital markets, America

tapped the global savings pool with extraordinary ease. Foreign capital helped finance
persistent federal budget and current account deficits while keeping U.S. borrowing costs
remarkably low. Washington could spend more than it collected, households could consume
more than they earned, and the country as a whole could invest more than it saved.
For decades, the world was happy to finance the difference. That comfortable
arrangement is now being challenged. At precisely the moment when America's financing

needs have rarely been greater, the age of abundant capital is giving way to something
very different: The age of capital competition.
Importantly, the world is not running out of savings. According to the International
Monetary Fund, gross global savings remain around 27% of world GDP , roughly in line
with the average of this century. The supply of savings has not collapsed.
What has changed is the other side of the equation. The world has developed an

enormous appetite for capital. Governments, corporations and households are increasingly
competing for essentially the same pool of savings. And when demand for something
rises faster than its supply, its price tends to rise. For capital, that price is the interest rate.
The past was different. The previous era of abundance was unusual not merely because
the world saved a great deal but because investment demand was comparatively weak.
China, Germany, Japan, oil-producing nations and other surplus economies generated

enormous pools of excess savings. Emerging market central banks accumulated foreign
exchange reserves, much of which found its way into U.S. Treasury securities. Meanwhile,
following the global financial crisis, private investment across much of the developed
world remained subdued.
The upshot: Too much capital was chasing too few attractive and potential investment
opportunities.
Today, that equation is reversing. Global savings remain plentiful, but the list of claimants

on that capital is exploding. We call them the Five Ds: Debt, Defense, Digitalization,
Demographics and Deglobalization.
Start with debt. Governments across the developed world are running large structural
deficits while simultaneously confronting rising interest expenses. The U.S. stands
out. Federal debt held by the public is approaching the size of annual U.S. GDP , and
Washington must finance large new deficits while continually refinancing trillions of

dollars of existing obligations (Exhibits 3 and 4). Treasurys are therefore competing for
capital on a massive scale.
Then comes defense. Russia's invasion of Ukraine, tensions in the Middle East and
strategic competition between the U.S. and China have ended the post-Cold War peace
dividend. America is rebuilding munitions inventories, Europe is rearming, and Asian
Investment Implications
The age of capital competition will likely
favor quality companies with strong

balance sheets, durable cash flows and
capital discipline, in our view. Investors
should consider from the global
capital spending cycle via exposure to
themes such as power and electrical
infrastructure, defense, robotics,
semiconductors and data centers.

### Page 5

5 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
nations are increasing military expenditures. Ships, missiles, satellites, drones and
defense-production facilities all require capital.
Third is digitalization. The AI revolution may prove to be among the most capital-
intensive technological transformations in history. Technology companies are spending
extraordinary sums on data centers, advanced semiconductors, networking equipment

and power generation. Utilities need capital to expand and modernize electrical grids.
Semiconductor manufacturers require tens of billions of dollars to build advanced
fabrication plants. All the above is enormously expensive and demands capital.
Fourth is demographics. Aging populations are placing growing pressure on
government budgets through pensions, healthcare and other social programs. Fewer
workers relative to retirees mean greater fiscal burdens across much of the developed

world. In the U.S. alone, mandatory spending outlays on Medicaid, Medicare and Social
Security have jumped from $724 billion in 2000 to $3.2 trillion in FY 2025.
Finally, there is deglobalization. For decades, globalization was fundamentally about
efficiency: Produce goods wherever costs were lowest. Today's world increasingly prizes
resilience and national security, which means the duplication of supply chains, reshored
factories, expanding strategic inventories and subsidized domestic manufacturing

facilities. All these objectives, of course, require capital.
Indeed, the common denominator of debt, defense, digitalization, demographics and
deglobalization is access to capital. And since these factors are structural rather than
cyclical, their demands on global savings are unlikely to fade any time soon.
Evidence of this emerging competition is increasingly visible in financial markets.
Investment-grade companies have already sold nearly $1.5 trillion of bonds this year,

roughly 36% more than during the comparable period last year and on pace to challenge
previous records, according to Bloomberg. With hyperscalers expected to continue
pouring capital into AI infrastructure through the end of the decade, corporate demand
for financing could remain elevated for years.
Meanwhile, the U.S. Treasury must finance enormous federal deficits. The result is an
increasingly crowded marketplace in which governments and corporations are competing

for the same investor dollars. Recent shifts by investment grade bond funds away from
Treasurys and toward corporate debt offer a small but telling glimpse of what this new
environment may look like (Exhibits 5 and 6).
For investors, the implications are significant. First, real interest rates may remain
structurally higher than during much of the previous two decades. Investors waiting for a
return to the ultra-low-rate environment of the 2010s may be waiting a very long time.

Second, fiscal policy matters again. In an era of abundant capital, markets often tolerated
fiscal excess. In a world of greater capital competition, countries that borrow heavily
without generating corresponding economic returns may eventually pay a higher price.
Keep a close eye on Japan.
Third, capital allocation matters more. Companies capable of generating strong returns
on invested capital, maintaining healthy balance sheets and funding expansion internally

should command a premium. When money is no longer virtually free, the difference
between productive investment and financial engineering becomes much more important.
Fourth, the world's enormous capital spending cycle should create winners. Power
generation, electrical infrastructure, defense, industrial automation, semiconductors, data
centers and other capital-intensive industries could enjoy years of elevated investment
and higher-than-expected earnings growth, in our view.

In the end, the age of capital abundance is fading—the era whereby capital basically
chased borrowers is past. Prologue will be defined by borrowers chasing capital. The tide
has turned.

### Page 6

6 of 8 August 31, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What portfolio addition could potentially help mitigate a sharp rise in inflation and/or yields?
How about the start of a new hiking cycle where short-term interest rates rise consistently?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Officer
• If inflation or a weaker U.S. dollar are the reason for a sharp rise in yields, then
increasing exposure to Commodities as a complement to a core portfolio makes sense.

Above-average inflation that is consistent and not excessive mixed with a weaker
U.S. dollar are generally tailwinds for commodities and the companies that mine for
them. Additionally, if global economic growth is running above trend and the U.S. dollar
weakens as it normalizes relative to other currencies, then adding non-U.S Developed
Markets and Emerging Markets (EM) exposure also makes sense.
• If short rates rise sharply due to a new interest rate hiking cycle, a more defensive tilt

in asset exposure makes sense. In other words, in the equity markets more defensive
sectors become more in demand, lower-volatility and higher-quality areas become
more in demand, cyclical shares tend to feel downward pressure, and highly levered
areas without a ton of debt are generally impacted the most. If the short-term rate
rise is minimal and a new hiking cycle is not beginning, then a solid mixture of Value
and Growth Equities, laddered portfolio in bonds, exposure to commodities and

thematic ideas make the most sense overall, in our view.

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

Personal Consumption Expenditures Price Index is a key economic measure that tracks the average change in prices paid by U.S. consumers for goods and services.
Citi Economic Surprise Index is an economic indicator that measures whether published macroeconomic data is beating or missing market expectations.
Consumer Price Index is a measure of the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative market basket of consumer goods and services.

conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.

## Document 247: Federal Reserve hikes 25bp, suggests another to come

Source PDF: all report pdf/Federal Reserve hikes 25bp, suggests another to come.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Article | 16 September 2026 1
THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article  |  16 September 2026   FX   RATES   UNITED STATES
Federal Reserve hikes 25bp, suggests
another to come
The Federal Reserve raised the policy rate 25bp and signalled they may
raise rates a second time before a long hold throughout 2027.
Nonetheless, we see more labour slack than the Fed and remain a little
more optimistic on the inflation backdrop. If we see a resumption of

energy flows from the Persian Gulf, the situation would favour a "one
and done" outcome
Fed Chair Warsh at today's press conference in which interest rates were raised by 25bp
Fed hike to "support a timelier return" of inflation to target
The Federal Reserve has hiked rates 25bp to a target range of 3.75–4%, as widely expected.
Markets had moved to swiftly price such an outcome in the wake of Chair Kevin Warsh’s
hawkish Jackson Hole address, with subsequent hotter-than-anticipated jobs and core inflation

data merely making it a formality.
The decision was unanimous and the press release was brief, as it has been after all three
decisions under Warsh’s leadership. Economic activity is “solid”, but uncertainty and inflation
remain “elevated”. Nonetheless, today’s decisions will “support a timelier return to the
Committee's 2 percent goal”.

### Page 2

THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article | 16 September 2026 2
Fed funds ceiling rate (%)
Source: Macrobond, ING
Fed points to one more hike followed by a long hold
In terms of the guidance, the central projection within the Fed’s summary of economic
projections has one more rate hike this year, in line with market pricing, with the central range
holding at 4.125% through 2027. They then have the mid-point of the Fed’s funds target range
at 3.875% for 2028 and 3.625% for end-2029 and 3.25% for the “longer run”. This is a higher

profile range than they projected in June, but is below the market pricing of three additional
25bp hikes.
Federal Reserve's economic projections
Source: Federal Reserve, ING
Within the actual dot plot there are only 18 submissions, suggesting Kevin Warsh again
declined to offer a view. Only 2 of the 18 FOMC submissions had no further rate hike this year,
suggesting strong backing for another move, and similarly, there are only 4 of 18 that expect

to see any sort of rate cut next year. There are 8 of 18 expecting the policy rate mid-point to
be 4.375% (2 additional hikes) by end-2027. So this is a more hawkish Fed than in June with
them revising up inflation and growth and revising lower unemployment projections despite

### Page 3

THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article | 16 September 2026 3
the data trends suggesting it should be the other way round - in our view.
Indeed, our growth, inflation and jobs forecasts suggest little need for further rate hikes, and it
may well be that the Fed are striking a hawkish line in order to build more credibility with bond
markets as they look to support Treasury efforts to anchor the long end of the curve.
One and done remains our call

Ordinarily the assumption is that if the Fed hikes, they don’t move just once, and indeed their
forecast table does have a further hike pencilled in. However, this time around we think it may
end up being a one-off. While August’s jobs number was a healthy 162,000, between January
2025 and July 2026 the monthly average increase was a measly 31k with hiring indicators
suggesting a reversion to that far slower growth rate is likely. Furthermore, while the

unemployment rate is low at just 4.1%, the worker participation rate has plunged a full
percentage point since early 2025. This suggests there is worker disengagement and more
slack in the jobs market than looking at the unemployment rate alone indicates. It is hard to
argue against that when wage growth is running at just 3%.
That weak wage growth will be helpful in keeping inflation in check and with the inflationary
impulse from tariffs largely incorporated – and IEEPA tariff refunds being a cash flow boost –

lower cost pressures here help mitigate higher energy and fuel costs. Furthermore, the shelter
component, with a 35% weighting in the CPI basket, should continue to moderate given the
stagnant property market and cooling private sector rents. The key uncertainty is energy and
the lack of progress surrounding oil and gas flows from the Strait of Hormuz, which has meant
headline disinflation has stalled. Nonetheless, our geopolitical assessment is that the situation

will improve, and oil prices will resume their declines later in the year, which will rapidly
improve the inflation backdrop.
Given market and consumer inflation expectations remain in check, we see parallels with the
late 1990s – cuts in early 1996 before a pause, then a one-off 'risk management' hike enacted
by Alan Greenspan’s Fed in March 1997 before a long pause through late 1998.
The Fed calms the back end, but we’d not be getting too
comfortable on it ahead

Ahead, we maintain a bearish stance on long rates despite the calmness post the decision, as
a 25bp hike does not materially change the dynamics that have hampered long rates in recent
months. Inflation remains high, as does the fiscal deficit, as is wider issuance. And the AI
productivity-driven narrative remains in place. The odds see the 10yr yield breaking back
above 5% in the days and weeks ahead. If so, the market will begin to settle at above 5%, and

ponder the 5.25% to 5.5% range as an area that is perfectly attainable in light of the still quite
loud mood music that has been driving long-end yields.

### Page 4

THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article | 16 September 2026 4
Delivery of the anticipated 25bp hike, by definition, should not have a material effect. The back
end initially took the decision very fine, with yields steady in the 4.95% area, although it had
shown a mild bias to test lower, as had been the theme through the morning into the decision.
What helped here was the price action of previous days that saw the 10yr yield get above 5%,
and indeed close above 5%, thus ticking off the need to necessarily have that reaction post

this decision. Chair Warsh will be pleased that the breakout of the 10yr yield shows a
moderate fall in inflation expectations, which telegraphs a nod of approval from the market to
the hike as an inflation containment one. The 10yr real yield is a tad higher as an offset.
Also, the 30yr yield is a tad richer vs SOFR, although not by much. Even though the 30yr yield
is still higher than it was before Treasury Secretary Bessent's buyback announcement, it’s well

below the subsequent highs. And it’s clinging on to a 4bp richening versus SOFR compared with
the pre-buyback announcement level. At the other end of the curve, the 2yr was a tad
spooked by the unanimity shown by the committee on the hike (Chair Warsh voted for the
hike too), and the implied priming for another hike from the dot plot. So, the 2yr yield is up
10bp to almost 4.7% post the decision. And the curve is flatter, mostly from the front end, and

the 2/10yr Treasury yield curve is back below 30bp. The 5yr is flat on the 2/5/10yr fly though,
suggesting that if there are more hikes, it should not be many.
Nothing of note on the plumbing, apart from noting ample bank reserves, suggesting a degree
of comfort with balance sheet circumstances. Which is fair. We await the outcome of further
deliberations in this space by the end of 2026, with the yet-to-come prescribed action to be
taken from 2027.

FX: Hawkish hike reinforces dollar momentum
The Fed’s message of policy discipline is likely to raise the bar for de-basement trades to re-
emerge anytime soon. That should limit downside risks for the dollar, leaving it better placed
to benefit from supportive external drivers. As long as energy prices remain firm, we see little
reason to fade the current USD appreciation trend.
With the door still open to additional tightening, the dollar also retains upside risk around

upcoming data releases. Strong prints could bring hike expectations forward to October, where
markets currently price 11bp.
EUR/USD broke below 1.150 after the announcement, and there is little in the way of obvious
support before the 1.1330-1.1360 area, where the June decline found a floor. That is likely the
extent of near-term downside risk. Our forecast remains more optimistic, albeit heavily reliant
on an improvement in the Gulf situation and rapid oil decline by year-end. We target 1.150 for

end-September and a modest bump to 1.160 by year-end.

## Document 261: Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1. There are two themes dominating our investor conversations: the impact of AI and 
the rise in interest rates. The two issues are linked as demand for capital from both 
the private and public sectors increase. In the private sector a surge in capex 
spending to fund AI infrastructure has eaten into free cash flow and forced 
companies to raise more in debt and equity markets. Meanwhile, government 
borrowing needs have increased as priorities shift towards upgrading critical 
infrastructure, energy security and defense at a time when cyclical inflationary 
pressures driven by higher energy prices are also resulting in higher policy rates. The 
combination has pushed up the cost of capital. As recently as 2022, for example, 
30-year bond yields in Germany and Japan were close to zero (Exhibit 1). A rise in 
yields, together with more uncertainty (over geopolitics and the future impact of AI) 
have, in combination, pushed up the cost of capital.

2. This rise in yields comes after a near-record period of equity outperformance 
relative to bonds over 10-year holding periods (Exhibit 2).

issue that has caused the biggest impact in the recent past. As our US strategists note, 
stocks have usually generated positive returns alongside rising interest rates unless the 
pace of rising rates exceeded 2+ standard deviations and this is a pattern that is broadly

5. While US equities have driven the bulk of equity outperformance for much of the 
fifteen years post the financial crisis, the improvement in earnings across regions, and 
the sharper de-rating in the US, has resulted in equity performance being 
broader-based by geography. Since 2025, the US equity market has been the weakest of 
the major regions (Exhibit 6), a turnaround from the dominant trend since the financial 
crisis and increasing investor returns from a more geographically diversified portfolio.

6. Supportive profit growth has been accompanied by positive earnings revisions, with 
2026 and 2027 estimates moving higher across major regions. There are four broad 
areas which have been driving much of this profit growth. First, technology earnings 
continue to be very robust. Second, rising energy prices have pushed up profits in the 
commodity sector. Third, banks have generally enjoyed strong earnings backed by 
positive nominal GDP growth, steep yield curves and strong private sector balance 
sheets. Finally, sectors such as Industrials have benefited from the surge in AI capex 
spending which has spilled over into improved revenues for the ‘pick and shovels’ of AI 
infrastructure. The breadth of the earnings’ growth has also increased the opportunity 
for investors to diversify across sectors as well as countries.

7. While technology profit growth has remained strong, the surge in capex spending 
among the hyperscalers has increasingly eaten through their free cash flow (Exhibit 8) 
prompting companies to look for alternative sources of funding, in the credit and equity 
markets.  The capex spending growth for AA-rated issuers has been substantial: the 65% 
year-over-year growth in Q2 marks the 10th consecutive quarter that aggregate AA 
capex growth exceeds 35%. They have also turned to the convertible bond market 
where volume has also increased year to date reaching $135 billion in the US, with 
AI-related borrowers driving 44% of total issuance, on our estimates. 
Our Credit team raised their full-year US IG gross issuance forecast by $200bn to $2.3 
trillion and AI-related issuers account for a quarter of USD IG gross supply this year, 
pushing overall supply to a year-to-date record.

surge. Globally technology has seen its PE ratio moderate, falling below its 20-year 
median (Exhibit 10). Meanwhile, the spending of these companies has spilled over into 
better earnings and more visible cash flows in many other parts of the economy. 
Healthcare, Consumer Discretionary and Industrials are all more expensive in absolute

9. For these reasons, we have argued that Technology equities have not experienced a 
classic bubble because valuation levels are much lower than in previous financial 
bubbles. Nevertheless, the risk for investors is less about valuation and more whether 
there is an unsustainable earnings bubble. Bubbles in earnings, rather than valuations, 
have occurred in previous periods.  The Bank sector, for example, briefly became the 
biggest sector in the S&P 500 in the run up to the financial crisis of 2008/09 (Exhibit 11). 
Unlike the technology bubble of the late 1990s, or the Japanese bubble of the late 
1980s, Bank stocks did not experience a major valuation bubble at the time, but the 
surge in earnings that drove the sector’s outperformance proved to be unsustainable.

### Page 8

There are, however, important differences to the Tech sector today. The earnings of the 
banks were boosted by sharply rising leverage and the asset that they were financing – 
US real estate – did experience a valuation bubble. When the housing market collapsed, 
pushing economies into recession, it triggered a sharp fall in underlying earnings for the 
banking sector. There are important differences today. First, technology profits remain 
very robust, and balance sheets are strong overall. Second, interest coverage ratios for 
the aggregate S&P 500 and its median stock, for example, rank in the 99th and 68th 
percentiles relative to the past 20 years. Third, demand for compute is accelerating and 
outstripping supply; Microsoft recently declared their intent to triple data centre 
capacity in 6 years. The bottlenecks are increasingly power, land and data centre 
capacity rather than a lack of compute demand. At our annual Communacopia 
Technology Conference, Nvidia reiterated its 2030 AI TAM outlook to $3tn-$4tn and 
supply constraints continue to be broad based. Nonetheless, any slowdown in profit 
growth, in an environment of a much higher cost of capital, could put downward 
pressure on equity prices, reducing confidence in future cash flows across the ecosystem 
from the hyperscalers to the ‘pick and shovels’ that have been benefiting from the capex 
boom.  
10. Given the near-term risks in bond markets, we remain neutral on equities, alongside 
other asset classes on a 3-month horizon, but continue to be overweight equities over 
12 months. While profit growth is likely to slow from the recent strong pace, ongoing 
growth in nominal GDP should underpin equity markets and the earnings cycle 
propelling equity returns even without any valuation expansion from current levels and 
our economists continue to see a low risk of recession. As our asset allocation team has 
shown, earnings growth and ROE are likely to continue to be the key drivers of equity 
performance relative to bonds, and we expect these fundamentals to remain supportive 
for equity returns. Nevertheless, we continue to recommend well diversified equity 
exposure as opportunities continue to broaden across geographies, sectors and factors.

### Page 9

Stock correlations are at multi-year lows across the major equity markets (Exhibit 12).

Even within sectors like technology, correlations have fallen sharply (Exhibit 13). The 
‘magnificent seven’ that was long seen as a homogeneous group of technology winners 
has also seen significant dispersion of returns. Differentiation, diversification and alpha 
generation are likely to remain the key drivers of equity returns. With higher bond yields, 
valuations are not likely to rise making earnings the critical driver of returns for investors.

## Document 286: Oil and gas_ A fragile market equilibrium maintains elevated price levels (2026-09-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Oil and gas_ A fragile market equilibrium maintains elevated price levels (2026-09-03).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-03
Pages: 1
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Oil & gas: A fragile market equilibrium maintains elevated price levels
03/09/2026 6
August 2026: Crude oil prices stabilize at a
high level
August 2026: Persisting tensions on the
European gas market
August 2026: Heatwaves fuel wholesale
electricity prices inflation in Europe
Oil and gas markets remain volatile and followed different trajectories during August. The gas market does not benefit
from the buffers in place in the crude oil market. Oil prices have stabilised at a high level, widening the gap compared to

the 2022 crisis. While the increase in gas prices remains lower than that seen following Russia's invasion of Ukraine, the
pace of the price increase is high.
Oil: Brent oil prices stabilised at a high level during August, in the absence of further military escalation and supported by
increased flows passing through the Strait (bypass pipelines and trade shuttle (tanker convoy escorted by US navy)). The
spread between the Brent futures price and the physical price (Dated Brent) has remained narrow since early June, driven

by the use of bypass, the accelerated drawdown on OECD inventories (notably US SPRs), and declining Chinese oil
imports.
Gas: European spot gas prices (TTF) have risen significantly over the past month for two main reasons: 1/ the lack of
recovery in LNG flows exiting the Gulf (unlike crude oil), and 2/ increased competition in the LNG market between Europe,
which is replenishing its pre-winter stocks, and Asia, where demand remains high during the summer months.

Electricity: The rise in European wholesale electricity prices accelerated during the summer due to successive heatwaves.
These heatwaves increased electricity demand and, furthermore, nuclear power production was constrained by
heatwaves and drought conditions in certain Central European countries and in France to a lesser extent. In this context,
the reliance on more expensive resources (gas) is driving electricity prices upward.

## Document 315: The Main Event_ Trump, Xi, and the Art of the Truce (2026-09-15)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The Main Event_ Trump, Xi, and the Art of the Truce (2026-09-15).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-09-15
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank Research Institute

### Page 2

Miha Hribernik | The Main Event: Trump, Xi, and the Art of the Truce |  September 2026
Why a quiet summit is a win for markets. As in May, our base case for the 24 September summit between
presidents Trump and Xi is a meeting heavy on positive optics but light on deliverables. Though a breakthrough is
unlikely, a successful tactical summit amid strategic competition is the realistic best-case outcome: it should
extend top-level stability into H1 2027 and minimise near-term risks of decoupling, a security crisis, or a disruptive

trade war.
The 3 November US midterms could incentivise the Trump administration to seek deals, as voter cost-of-living
concerns are highest since April. Possible deliverables include progress on a Board of Trade for non-sensitive
goods, agricultural purchases, targeted investment deals, and an extension of the 2025 Busan agreement, which
postponed a range of mutual economic restrictions until November 2026. But even if not secured on the 24th, we

assess that an extension is likely before the deadline.
Economic and national security concerns will put a ceiling on dealmaking. We are cautious about progress on a
Board of Investment or broad investment pacts, despite an expected Chinese business delegation. Their presence
is more likely a reciprocal gesture to the May visit by US CEOs rather than a precursor to sweeping bilateral
agreements. Mutual national security concerns make structural breakthroughs difficult, and any potential deals –

such as Chinese automotive investment in the US – would likely face significant political and regulatory
headwinds.
Trade remains an unresolved bilateral issue. While tensions have subsided and the Board of Trade could ease
some tariffs, differences persist amid rebounding Chinese exports. Washington is also set to unveil new
overcapacity tariffs on 16 economies, including China, while seeking to restrict Chinese inputs in North American
supply chains and targeting alleged transshipment.

The AI race is moving too fast for summit diplomacy to slow it down. Both sides could reportedly hold a high-
level dialogue ahead of the summit. This would likely focus on areas of consensus, such as addressing misuse by
non-state actors. There is little prospect of slowing the escalating China-US AI race, and even less that talks could
reverse growing tech restrictions (see list).
Geopolitical issues will loom large, but policy shifts are unlikely. Differences over the war in Iran – including

recent US sanctions on dozens of mainland and Hong Kong entities – and Taiwan will likely feature, but
breakthroughs on either issue remain improbable. A key watchpoint is whether President Trump continues to seek
negotiating leverage by signalling further delays to arms sales to Taipei.
Washington summit should extend stability into H1 2027…
…but the underlying China-US economic squeeze is set to continue
Chinese exports to the US rebounded in August

After the summit: Key China and US watchpoints for Q4
Date Market(s) Comment
October China The Central Committee of the Communist Party of China will hold its Fifth Plenary Session. This will
be one of the most important events on the country’s political calendar ahead of the 21st Party
Congress to be held in late 2027. The Plenum will focus on Party governance in preparation for key

leadership reshuffles at the next Congress.
3 November US In the Congressional midterm elections, voters will decide 435 House seats, 35 Senate seats and
governors in 39 states and territories. These elections will determine Congressional control,
significantly influencing President Trump’s ability to shape national policy.
If a changed balance of power in Congress stalls domestic legislation, the administration will likely
lean on executive tools. President Trump could focus on foreign policy and trade, emphasising tariffs,

US primacy in the Western Hemisphere, and reducing dependencies on China to build his legacy.
10 November US-China The 2025 Busan agreement is set to expire, but will likely be extended ahead of the deadline. This
would delay new bilateral economic measures including expanded export controls and tariffs.
18-19
November
APEC China will host the APEC Economic Leaders’ meeting. The summit could see a Xi-Trump meeting on
the sidelines, and may shape regional economic architecture for years to come.

14-15
December
G20 The G20 Summit in Miami may become contentious due to Ukraine-related divisions, trade and tariff
disputes, climate financing disagreements and questions around global governance reforms. The
summit could also see this year’s fourth meeting between presidents Xi and Trump.
December China China’s annual Central Economic Work Conference will review past economic performance and set
2027 growth targets and macroeconomic policies. Key priorities likely include implementing the 15th

Five-Year Plan, boosting domestic demand, stabilising the property sector, and strengthening
technological self-reliance amid geopolitical competition with Washington.
December US As the US Congress prepares to adopt its annual must-pass defence legislation, draft provisions –
not all of which may end up in the final version – target China with measures impacting investors.
These include potential outbound-investment reporting changes (impacting biotechnology and

biopharmaceuticals) and proposed restrictions on importing humanoid robots and solar equipment, as
well as using China-linked connected vehicles.

## Document 349: “鹰派加息”！沃什“巨变”

Source PDF: all report pdf/“鹰派加息”！沃什“巨变”.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

美联储 9 ⽉全票加息 25 个基点，沃什以鹰派⾏动兑现鹰派承诺，并明确表⽰当前⾦融条件并
不紧缩、本次加息仅移除 “ 部分宽松 ” ，措辞强硬。瑞银认为，沃什的政策反应函数较前任发
⽣实质性转变 —— 对通胀和供给冲击更敏感，对劳动⼒市场的顾虑明显更低，且将限制性
政策的⻔槛设得更⾼。⻛险明显偏向利率更⻓时间维持⾼位。
摘要：
9 ⽉ 17 ⽇，美联储在 FOMC 会议上以 12 ⽐ 0 全票通过加息 25 个基点，将联邦基⾦利率⽬标区间调
升⾄ 3.75%-4.00% 。这是⼀次 “ ⾔⾏⼀致 ” 的鹰派加息：主席沃什在新闻发布会上的措辞，与他在
杰克逊霍尔会议上奠定的鹰派基调⾔论完全吻合。
据追⻛交易台消息，瑞银随即发布报告解读本次加息。报告显⽰，沃什在会后的新闻发布会上
不⽌⼀次强调，当前⾦融条件 “ 并不构成限制性 ” ，本次加息仅是移除了 “ 部分宽松 ” （ a dose of
accommodation ），措辞远⽐市场预期更强硬。
沃什明确拒绝将中性利率赋予任何 “ 操作意义 ” ，显⽰其政策框架已与前任主席拉开距离。点阵图

显⽰， FOMC 委员中位预测年内还将再加息⼀次，并将 4.1% 的利率中值维持贯穿整个 2027 年。
瑞银经济学家 Jonathan Pingle 及其团队评估，与过去三届乃⾄更多届美联储主席相⽐，沃什的
政策反应函数发⽣了实质性转变：对⾦融条件更敏感，对劳动⼒市场影响更不敏感，且将 “ 限制
性货币政策 ” 的⻔槛设得更⾼。这⼀判断，对未来四年的市场预期具有直接影响。
三⼤变量催⽣ “ 坚定⼀致 ” 的决定
沃什在发布会上披露了 7 ⽉以来影响决策的三个新变量，逐⼀拆解了本次加息的逻辑⽀撑。
第⼀，劳动⼒市场强劲， 8 ⽉就业报告给予有⼒印证。
第⼆，通胀趋势令⼈不安，近期 CPI 数据未能提供正⾯信号。
第三，地缘政治局势，及其对能源价格的影响。
沃什原话如是说： “第三个在七周内发⽣变化的事情是地缘政治。世界各地的热点局势⽆处遁
形，我们对地缘政治形势最可能或最不可能⾛向的判断已经改变。这三点共同指向了今天这个
坚定、⼀致的决定。 ”
他还特别提到，⾃就任以来与其他全球央⾏⾏⻓进⾏了多次交流 —— 这在以往美联储决策框架

中并不常⻅。分析师指出，沃什可能⽐前任主席更加关注全球央⾏之间的政策对话。
“ 不伤害劳动⼒市场 ” ，但沃什逻辑与众不同
沃什在会上表态： “ 我不认为我们需要损害劳动⼒市场来实现我们的⽬标。我不认为我们双重使
命的两个组成部分 —— 价格稳定和充分就业 —— 在中期内是相互对⽴的。 ”
价格稳定是经济增⻓的基础，我认为我们今天迈出了重要⼀步来实现它。我们部分通过移除这剂
宽松（ a dose of accommodation ）来做到这⼀点。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

这句话听起来温和，但报告的解读截然相反。
据研报，这并⾮ “ 劳动⼒市场看起来不错、通胀⼜太⾼ ” 这种常规逻辑，⽽是沃什认为更⾼的利率
根本不会实质性冲击就业扩张。他对劳动⼒市场冲击的容忍度 —— 或者说对此的漠然 —— 本⾝
就构成了其政策框架的核⼼特征之⼀。
换⾔之，沃什并⾮在说 “ 我会⼩⼼翼翼地保护就业 ” ，⽽更像是在说 “ 加息不会伤害就业，所以我
没有理由不加 ” 。
对供给冲击的敏感性：⼀个新的政策信号
分析师还特别指出，沃什似乎⽐前任主席对能源价格冲击更为敏感。
这或许源于其对美联储在新冠疫情时期应对失当的批判性反思。历史上，包括 2007-2008 年时的
联储内部，曾有观点认为应对油价上涨 “ 视⽽不⻅ ” （ look through ），因为能源冲击最终会⾃然消
退。沃什此前作为联储理事时，曾对 Charles Plosser 和 Richard Fisher 等⼈关注油价通胀⼆次效
应的⽴场表⽰同情。
如今⾝为主席，他的⽴场已然转变。沃什在会上提及 “ ⼆次效应 ” （ second round effects ） —— 这

⼀措辞更接近欧洲央⾏的政策语⾔，⽽⾮美联储传统的 “ 能源价格传导通胀 ” 讨论框架。
这意味着沃什倾向于在供给冲击⾯前主动出击，将通胀压回⽬标，⽽⾮等待冲击⾃然消退后再
调整。
因此据报告分析，与前任主席相⽐，沃什的反应函数呈现出三个显著特征：
第⼀，对⾦融条件更敏感。 他对市场的关注程度⾼于前任，但⽅向相反 —— 他对市场下跌表现
出明显的漠然，在杰克逊霍尔演讲中直接忽视，今天的发布会上也未表现出担忧。
第⼆，对劳动⼒市场更不敏感。 他的双重使命表述，实际上淡化了就业对货币政策的约束⼒。
第三，对 “ 限制性政策 ” 的⻔槛更⾼。 他拒绝承认当前利率⽔平具有限制性，这意味着他愿意将
利率推得更⾼、维持更久。
瑞银总结道：

### Page 3

点阵图：写下低利率，却按⾼利率⾏事
本次点阵图揭⽰了⼀个耐⼈寻味的内在⽭盾。
中位预测显⽰， FOMC 将利率⻓期均衡点（ longer-run dot ）从 3.1% 上调⾄ 3.2% 。然⽽，委员们
实际写下的利率路径 ——2027 年维持 4.1% ， 2028 年降⾄ 3.9% ， 2029 年再降⾄ 3.6%—— 意味着
在整个预测期内，名义政策利率始终⾼于这⼀⻓期均衡点。
这表明 “FOMC 实际上在按照⼀个⾼于其名义⻓期均衡利率的⽔平⾏事 ” ，换⾔之，委员们在内⼼
深处认为，在未来三⾄四年内，实现价格稳定所需的实际利率⽔平要更⾼。
此外，沃什本⼈再次未提交点阵——18 个数据点中只有 17 个延伸⾄ 2027 年以后。分析师认为，
“ 显然有⼀位参与者认为三年后的预测⼏乎没有实⽤价值 ” 。
在 SEP 通胀预测⽅⾯，今年核⼼ PCE 通胀预测从 3.3% 上调⾄ 3.4% ，略超 UBS 预期；但未来三年
路径与回归 2% ⽬标⼀致。值得注意的是，尽管利率预测⼤幅上调， GDP 增⻓预测也同步上修

——2026 年从 2.2% 升⾄ 2.3% ，失业率预测则从 4.3% ⼤幅下调⾄ 4.1% ，并预计在整个预测期内
都低于⻓期均衡⽔平 4.2% 。
瑞银的利率路径预测
据瑞银研究报告，其基准预测如下：
10 ⽉ FOMC：跳过加息（与 2026 年 6 ⽉⾄ 7 ⽉的观望模式⼀致，同时也避免在中期选举前六天
加息）
12 ⽉ FOMC：再加息 25 个基点
总体⽽⾔，与过去四⼗年相⽐，我们可能正⾯对⼀个鹰派程度更⾼的 FOMC 反应函数，⽽这⼀状
态将持续未来四年。

### Page 4

2027 年：维持不变， 6 ⽉开始降息
但该⾏明确表⽰，今天的发布会之后，这⼀利率路径的⻛险明显偏向上⾏。
沃什在发布会上对前瞻指引的态度也颇为直⽩：

更详细的解读，包括实时解读、⼀线研究等内容，请加⼊【追⻛交易台 ▪ 年度会员】
我的职责不是提供前瞻指引，但我在 6 ⽉的承诺是向美国⼈⺠、向所有倾听者重申，我们将实现
价格稳定 …… 今天的⾏动开始表明我们是认真的，我们将兑现价格稳定⽬标，并且正如声明所
说，我们将以更及时的⽅式做到这⼀点。

中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
